However, we observe substantial quality degradation when generating long-form, open-ended responses. We attribute this gap to query-guided selection, which can retain sufficient evidence for answer selection while discarding details needed for comprehensive generation. This motivates reducing overlapping information across tokens, termed structural redundancy, while preserving diverse content. However, the retained representations may still contain precision redundancy due to unnecessarily high numerical precision in their cached values. To address these two levels of redundancy in token representations, we introduce a training-free framework named R$^2$-Omni. First, Structure-Aware Token Consolidation (STC) consolidates redundant representations using spatiotemporal similarity in video and local continuity in audio. Then, Semantic-Sensitivity Guided Precision Allocation (SSPA) allocates value precision to the retained representations according to the estimated reduction in attention-output error under quantized keys, with query attention and audio-visual alignment providing a semantic prior. Extensive experiments on long-form generation benchmarks demonstrate an average 5.0$\times$ reduction in the memory footprint while preserving 97.98% of baseline generation quality and achieving a 1.48$\times$ end-to-end speedup, highlighting the benefits of jointly reducing structural and precision redundancy.</p></details> | 32 pages, 9 figures |
| **[Cold-Start Pricing of LLM Services under Two-Sided Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03199v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Online large language model (LLM) services face a pricing cold start: providers initially lack the historical data and user information needed for precise pricing, while users' willingness to pay evolves with repeated use. We formulate this challenge as a \emph{two-sided learning} problem between a provider and a user. The user learns from private usage experience, whereas the provider incurs a serving cost and dynamically adapts prices using only purchase decisions to maximize cumulative profit. The provider's central challenge is to learn to price against an evolving willingness to pay without observing the private experiences driving that evolution. We establish matching upper and lower bounds on the minimax regret over horizon $T$, identifying two cost-dependent regimes. When serving costs are sufficiently low, our Calibration-and-Tracking (CAT) algorithm achieves the optimal rate $Θ(T^{1/3})$. In the higher-cost regime, where the provider must learn whether continued service is profitable, Calibration-and-Tracking with Retirement (CAT-R) attains the optimal rate $Θ(\sqrt{T})$. This phase transition shows that serving costs affect not only profit margins but also the fundamental difficulty of learning to price LLM services. Simulations support the predicted regret scaling and show that our algorithms outperform standard online learning baselines in the evaluated settings.</p></details> |  |
| **[Finding the Pivots to Repair Broken Source-Test Evolution Using LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03197v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Repairing obsolete tests in response to source code evolution is critical for test suite reliability and software quality assurance. While large language models (LLMs) have emerged as promising tools for automating many software development tasks, the effective supply of context and the orchestration of repair pipelines in this source--test co-evolution scenario remain poorly understood. In this paper, we present a systematic empirical study of test repair for source-test co-evolution empowered by LLMs, investigating the effective setups and typical LLM behaviors. To support this study, we construct a new benchmark of 500 real-world source-test co-evolution cases from commits submitted between May 2024 and April 2026 across 15 highly-starred open-source Java projects. Our experimental results reveal several key insights for effective LLM-based test repair: the complementary benefits of combining test failure symptoms, source code changes, and supportive code context, as well as the effectiveness of self-repair rounds. We further derive a common reasoning action taxonomy from LLMs' reasoning traces, which proves instrumental in guiding LLMs to repair tests more effectively while at a lower financial cost. Applying our findings to the Codex agent further improves its repair success rate on challenging cases by 66.7%.</p></details> |  |
| **[Verification Allocation in Stylized LLM Agent Pipelines under Hidden Tool Degradation: A Discrete-Time Mean-Field Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03192v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We study a stylized verification-allocation model for role-structured pipelines with binary inherited corruption and hidden tool degradation. We characterize a two-action threshold under an explicit monotonicity condition and a restricted four-action interval and tail policy when generation rates are equal and only one check degrades. Code and results: https://github.com/Shereen20112003/verification-allocation-hidden-tool-degradation</p></details> |
| **[Controlling Sharpening in LLM Post-Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03146v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM post-training via reinforcement learning has shown consistent improvements in accuracy at the cost of reducing behavioral coverage - a phenomenon known as sharpening that can stall progress on difficult tasks. Repeated sampling objectives such as $\mathrm{pass@k}$ and $\mathrm{max@k}$ are known to mitigate sharpening, but the mechanism behind it remains poorly understood. Moreover, existing approaches for $\mathrm{@k}$ optimization use dedicated algorithms, and no general framework exists for adapting established policy optimization methods from $\mathrm{@}1$ to $\mathrm{@k}$. We derive the optimal policies for KL-regularized $\mathrm{pass@k}$ and $\mathrm{max@k}$ objectives in closed form. Our explicit solutions reveal that $\mathrm{@k}$ optimization naturally redistributes learning pressure based on prompt difficulty, directing it toward harder problems. Crucially, we show that standard $\mathrm{@}1$ objectives achieve these exact same optimal policies through prompt-adaptive regularization or reward transformation. Ultimately, this yields an optimizer-independent explanation for why $\mathrm{@k}$ objectives reduce sharpening on easy tasks while prioritizing success on harder ones. Building on these insights, we introduce $\mathrm{@k}$ Adaptive Pressure for Policy Optimization (KAPPO). Following a one-time reference calibration, KAPPO seamlessly adapts existing $\mathrm{@}1$ methods to $\mathrm{@k}$ via prompt-adaptive regularization or reward transformation, without otherwise altering their training procedures. | **[From Local Skills to Long-Horizon Web Tasks: Progressive Skill Exploration for LLM Web Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03116v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) are driving autonomous agents toward real-world environments, with the Web emerging as one of the most representative interaction scenarios. However, training Web agents with naive exploration remains challenging: successful trajectories are rare, and the sparse terminal rewards make it difficult for agents to obtain useful feedback from failed trajectories. Through this process, SCOPE progressively constructs task-completing skill chains across rollouts and internalizes them into the policy parameters through agentic on-policy distillation. Extensive experiments on WebArena, WebVoyager, and WebShop show that SCOPE consistently improves exploration dynamics and achieves 3.9% to 18.6% relative gains over the strongest baselines in each setting.</p></details> | <details><summary>31 pa...</summary><p>31 pages, 9 figures. Accepted at NeurIPS 2026</p></details> |

## Agent
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |

| **[From Autonomous Agents to Cognitive Symbionts: Toward AI-Augmented Human Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05376v1)** | 2026-11-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>For a decade, progress in artificial intelligence has been measured by how much a machine can do on its own. Large foundation models now write code and run multistep tasks, and personal agents act on a person's behalf. Yet people still forget what they once knew, miss what they are not looking at, fail to find the right words, and face hard choices without the benefit of their own history, because the machine that could help stands outside the situation. As AI moves into the physical world, attention has settled on one kind of intelligent individual, the embodied robot; we argue that the era of Physical AI holds a second kind of equal importance, the person augmented by AI. Alongside artificial general intelligence, which asks how intelligent a machine can be, stands AI-augmented human intelligence (AHI), which asks how much intelligence a machine can enable in a particular person. Realizing it calls for a new kind of system, a cognitive symbiont: a lifelong, embedded, real-time, private, low-friction, and proactive companion that senses what the person senses through external devices, keeps their context across years, models their situation and their mind, takes part in their thinking at the right moment, carries out everyday tasks with their context, and keeps learning the person as both change. We give the paradigm a conceptual core, the cognitive gap between what a person could bring to a situation and what they can hold in working memory, and organize its design space as a symbiosis loop of sensing, keeping, understanding, living alongside, acting for and thinking with the person, and growing, enclosed by a personal runtime and by trust. Because a symbiont changes what a person can do in front of others, it also raises risks of a new kind, from covert assistance in examinations to pressure on everyone to adopt it, and we treat their governance as part of the paradigm.</p></details> | <details><summary>59 pa...</summary><p>59 pages, 21 figures, 18 tables. Project page: https://cognitive-symbiont.github.io</p></details> |
| **[Almost-MMS allocations with small agent redundancy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05360v1)** | 2026-11-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>For a fixed $ε\ge 0$, let $ρ_{ε, n}$ be the maximum value of $ρ_n$ such that, in every allocation instance with $m$ indivisible goods and $(1 + ε)n$ agents, there is a subset of $n$ agents with the following property. The allocation instance restricted to only this subset of $n$ agents has an allocation that gives each agent at least $ρ_n$-fraction of her $n$-maximin share (MMS). When $ε> 0$ we refer to $εn$ as the {\em agent redundancy}. The case $ε= 0$ is the standard setting without agent redundancy, and we use $ρ_{0,n}$ and $ρ_n$ interchangeably. For allocation instances with additive valuations, % agents, it is known that $\frac{7}{9} \le ρ_n < 1-Ω(\frac{1}{\log^2 n})$ for every $n \ge 2$ (with stronger lower bounds for small $n$). It is an open question whether $ρ_n$ tends to~1 as $n$ grows. We show that with agent redundancy, the answer to this question is positive. That is, for every $ε> 0$, $ρ_{ε, n}$ tends to~1 as $n$ grows. A straightforward corollary of this result is that for every $c > 0$, if the number of items satisfies $m \le (2 - c)n$, then the answer is positive also for $ρ_n$ (with no agent redundancy). Adapting our techniques to a related setting, we also improve a recent result of Ezra and Garbuz, and show that without agent redundancy, there always exists an allocation that gives $n - O(\log^2 n)$ agents their full MMS.</p></details> |  |
| **[Adversarial Speculative Reasoning Improves Long Horizon Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05345v1)** | 2026-11-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Speculative decoding has been treated purely as a systems optimization that speeds up large language model decoding without changing the model behavior. Under this view, the growing effort to train better draft models has been focused solely on acceptance rate. We argue that this view does not hold true for long-horizon agent reasoning. Measuring frontier open-weight models on complex agentic tasks with different speculative decoding approaches, we found that numerical noise grows within verification windows, and the draft acceptance results of the thinking process can change the output distribution of the model after 30 steps, thus noticeably influencing the reasoning accuracy of long-horizon agents in random directions. In this work, we improve the reasoning capability of target models by controlling such noise. While we cannot change the overall token distribution verified by the target model, we can encode critical reasoning with highest precision by training the draft model to be rejected. We identify these points in agent traces and train the draft model to favor adversarial preference. At inference the trained draft and the target part ways precisely at these decision points, verification restarts, so that critical thinking tokens are sampled from the target model and encoded under its least noisy conditions. This approach makes critical reasoning better indexed and more durable over a long horizon. On complex agentic coding benchmarks, our approach raises end-to-end success rate with no change to the target model or its output distribution. Our results recast the speculative decoding mechanism from a pure accelerator into an active allocator for context precision that improves the accuracy of long-horizon agents by training 0.06% to 0.28% of hosted parameters.</p></details> |  |
| **[trajectory-judge: What Outcome-Only LLM Judges Miss on Agent Trajectories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00038v4)** | 2026-11-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>A direct test of an LLM judge of agent trajectories injects faults into correct runs and reports recall, per fault type or by whether the fault broke the environment outcome (loud) or not (silent). Such recall can credit a judge with detection it does not have; paired discrimination, its flag rate on the faults minus its rate on the clean runs they came from, exposes this. Our testbed, a deterministic support desk with a scripted oracle and a one-step fault injector, labels all 400 trajectories exactly. A 14B judge shown only the request and final reply scores 34% to 76% recall on four fault types that leave the reply unchanged. There its input is the clean run's, so its paired discrimination is zero and that recall is its flag rate on clean runs. Splitting by outcome survival does not fix this: its loud recall of 84% is a paired +0.393 and its silent recall of 45% a paired +0.048, all from the two fault types that change the reply. Told to check each step, the same model flags every fault of those four types and 0 of 100 clean runs (95% CI up to 3.6%). It does not reliably check the reply: of four invented promises it flags one every time and the other three once in 42 faults. Shown every step but asked only about the reply, it still reaches a paired +0.69 on reply-unchanged faults, against +1.00 when told to check each step. We recommend reporting paired discrimination against clean parents, split by whether the fault reaches the judge's input and by outcome survival, and release the testbed, raw verdicts and analysis pipeline.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at the NeurIPS 2026 Workshop: Who Verifies the Agents? Toward Reliable Agent Development (poster). Camera-ready version. 22 pages, 5 figures, 14 tables. Code and data: https://github.com/mohammadi-hadi/trajectory-judge</p></details> |
| **[Pretraining Knows What, Simulation Teaches How: Compositional Generalization for Agentic Robotics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35817v3)** | 2026-11-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Frontier models such as GPT-6 Astra now directly control robots and can generalize better than vision-language-action models (VLAs). We argue that this ability could arise from two distinct, composable skills: semantic reasoning, to understand what a task asks for in a scene, and motion control, to move the robot to do it. We show that small open VLMs are already capable of the first and mainly lack the second. We introduce Zero-real Agent Policy (ZAP), a recipe that teaches this missing motion skill, entirely in simulation on simple primitive tasks and from the model's own successes, with no frontier teacher or distillation and no real-world robot data. ZAP reaches 96.7% success on real-world tasks, matching GPT-6 Astra in our harness while running 3x faster and 17x cheaper, and 74.0% on a bimanual adaptation of RoboLab-120, on par with GPT-6 Astra's 72.2%. ZAP also generalizes to new objects, goals, and strategies, and in simulation to a robot embodiment on which it never trained.</p></details> |  |
| **[PyRUA-DataFarm: LLM Agent as a Cost-Efficient Teleoperator for Real-Ready Robot Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05320v1)** | 2026-11-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Teleoperation remains the default way to collect robot demonstrations, but every episode costs robot and operator time, and every new task starts from scratch. We ask whether a coding agent can take over the teleoperator's job. In our DataFarm, the agent receives a data request and one calibration episode of the robot in the target scene and rebuilds the task in simulation: it writes the scene, an expert controller, and a success judge. The simulator supplies exact actions, and a video model conditioned on real images renders each rollout as photographic video, generating all camera views together consistently. The output is real-ready data: synchronized camera views labelled with the robot's actions, on which a policy can be trained directly and deployed on the real robot. Independent judges certify the expert and every rendered episode before admission. On five real-robot tasks, policies trained only on farm data match policies trained on as many teleoperated episodes (93 vs. 84 successes in 100 trials), and under an unseen tablecloth and coloured light they keep 87 successes where the teleoperation-trained policies fall to 46. Adapting a task is fully automatic and costs \$21 of API usage on average, paid once per task, plus \$5 for a render contract; every further episode costs \$0.80 on average for simulation, rendering, and review, with no robot or operator. One hour of three-view data for a task costs \$107 on average, these costs included, slightly below a quote of \$117 for teleoperation in China and below \$290 in labour alone in North America.</p></details> | <details><summary>22 pa...</summary><p>22 pages, 9 figures, 9 tables. Project page: https://dagroup-pku.github.io/PyRUA-Lean/DataFarm Code: https://github.com/DAGroup-PKU/PyRUA-Lean/tree/DataFarm</p></details> |
| **[HARP: Training-Free Dual-Profile Agentic Communication for LLM-Based Recommendation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05319v1)** | 2026-11-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In LLM-based recommendation, memory acts as a user-level agent in a memory-augmented backbone. Yet users and items remain asymmetric: only the user is given a structured profile; it enters a bandwidth-limited reranker prompt; and it is frozen after warmup. We recast LLM-based recommendation as an agentic-communication process in four stages: BUILD a self-description, TRANSMIT it along the collaborative graph, UNDERSTAND the other side, and SYNC it from the next interaction. This formulation reveals three unresolved communication challenges: the item side never produces a comparable self-description (one-sided BUILD); profile-level signals enter only the bandwidth-limited reranker prompt rather than retrieval, where they would not compete for context tokens (prompt-trapped UNDERSTAND); and the user's description is frozen after warmup with no principled update rule (absent SYNC). We instantiate the missing stages as HARP (Homophily-aware Agentic Recommendation via dual Profiles), a training-free, three-operator addition to a frozen LLM backbone: a rule-extracted symmetric item profile (zero extra LLM calls), a channel-decomposable profile-level homophily H(phi_u, phi_i) consumed at retrieval time rather than inside the reranker prompt, and a single-pass reflective update realising in-context profile evolution without any gradient step. On four public benchmarks (Yelp, Books, MovieTV, Goodreads), HARP lifts Hit@1 over the strongest published baseline by 6.9%-24.0%, with family-wise Holm-corrected p < 1e-9 on every dataset. Ablations and a per-case attribution study show that the operators contribute complementary gains, with the retrieval-side gate as the main source; we also report the trade-off introduced by the reflective pass. We formulate agentic communication and provide a training-free instantiation. Code is available at https://github.com/Jove-Zhen/HARP.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted by NeurIPS 2026</p></details> |
| **[MDSKILL: Learning to Internalize Skill Memory through Multi-View Distillation for Efficient Dynamic Agent Self-Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05313v1)** | 2026-11-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Skill memories encode past experiences as textual prompts to improve reinforcement learning (RL) for self-evolving agents. OPSD then transfers and consolidates complementary experiential knowledge across these branches into a shared policy, enabling efficient skill internalization and continuous agent self-evolution. We evaluate MDSKILL on three agent benchmarks against RL, skill-augmented, and self-distillation baselines that do not rely on stronger external teacher models. Without additional computational overhead, MDSKILL improves upon SOTA performance by up to 7.1% and 8.1% on ALFWorld and WebShop. | **[Democratizing Robot-Use Agents: A Unified Harness with Open Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01560v2)** | 2026-11-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Robot-use Agents (RUAs) have shown promising zero-shot manipulation capabilities, but reliance on frontier, proprietary models limits research into further training. | **[ToolSched: Resource-Efficient Tool Scheduling for Shared-Host LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05269v1)** | 2026-11-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Local LLM agents often work on shared hosts. ToolSched embeds a resource model into the MCP interface: agents estimate resource demands along with command requests. These hints, together with real-time host resource conditions, determine when commands are actually executed. Internally, ToolSched applies a scheduling algorithm to stagger commands, reducing contention and improving resource utilization. Prediction correction and checkpoint-based recovery mitigate estimation bias and resource errors. Our evaluation on Terminal Bench shows that, compared with unscheduled execution, ToolSched improves resource utilization by 148.4% and end-to-end command throughput by 29.4%, demonstrating its effectiveness and practicality.</p></details> |  |
| **[Stabilizing On-Policy Targeted Guidance in Agentic Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05266v1)** | 2026-11-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Naive on-policy RL is often bottlenecked by the capabilities of the base model --- insufficient coverage can result in an inability to learn from certain rewards. | **[JHiMA-WebRL: A Joint Hierarchical Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning Framework for Long-Horizon Web Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05263v1)** | 2026-11-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal web agents have shown promising capabilities for interacting with websites through graphical user interfaces (GUIs), yet realistic web tasks often require long-horizon, multi-step planning that remains challenging for monolithic policies. | **[SEEK-VAU: Towards Evidence-Faithful Video Anomaly Understanding via Agentic Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04554v1)** | 2026-11-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Most video anomaly understanding (VAU) systems still infer anomaly judgments from evidence collected in a feed-forward way under predefined rules, typically sampled frames, or events clustered from such frames. Hardware profilers report how busy each unit was, not where each warp's time went; intra-kernel tracers can show it, but only with hand-placed probes and a recompilation that alters the very kernel being measured. We present Warpline, which gives agents this view automatically while leaving the kernel essentially unchanged. Warpline analyzes the binary to decide which events to record and inserts probes into the binary itself without recompilation or any manual step. It then aligns the trace with hardware profiling data on one unified timeline. Because it needs only the binary, it can regenerate this evidence for every new version of the kernel at whatever level the agent edits it (source, IR, or PTX), closing the optimization loop without human intervention. On NVIDIA B200, across 35 kernels in Triton, CuTe DSL, and CUDA C++, Warpline adds a median overhead of 1.2% and leaves a median 99.9% of executed instructions unchanged, whereas existing tracers have median overheads of 1.6% to 13.2% and leave only 4.6% to 32.7% unchanged. Warpline also traces closed-source kernels, which these tracers cannot. On 10 SOL-ExecBench problems, agents using Warpline spend less time and fewer model requests than a bare coding agent and NVIDIA's Kernel Design Agents (KDA), yet lead both after every round in geometric mean and reach a 7.69$\times$ geometric-mean speedup, against 6.94$\times$ and 6.13$\times$. Two case studies further demonstrate that Warpline reveals hidden bottlenecks and provides actionable optimization insights.</p></details> | <details><summary>14 pa...</summary><p>14 pages, 7 figures, 2 tables</p></details> |
| **[A Mimamsa Inspired Framework For Instruction Sequencing In AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.17691v2)** | 2026-11-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>This paper presents a formal framework for sequencing instructions in AI agents, inspired by the Indian philosophical system of Mimamsa. The framework formalizes sequencing mechanisms through action object pairs in three distinct ways: direct assertion (Srutikrama) for temporal precedence, purpose driven sequencing (Arthakrama) for functional dependencies, and iterative procedures (Pravrittikrama) for distinguishing between parallel and sequential execution in repetitive tasks. It introduces the syntax and semantics of an action object imperative logic, extending the MIRA formalism (Srinivasan and Parthasarathi, 2021) with explicit deduction rules for sequencing. The correctness of instruction sequencing is established through a validated theorem, which is based on object dependencies across successive instructions. This is further supported by proofs of soundness and completeness. This formal verification enables reliable instruction sequencing, impacting AI applications across areas like task planning and robotics by addressing temporal reasoning and dependency modeling.</p></details> | <details><summary>Revis...</summary><p>Revision of this paper is in progress</p></details> |
| **[ATC: Agent Trajectory Control for Role-Contract Enforcement in Black-Box Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04321v1)** | 2026-11-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-agent LLM systems assign different responsibilities to different agents: one plans, another retrieves evidence, another verifies, another executes. Over many turns, however, an agent can produce a locally reasonable message that exercises authority belonging to another role: a verifier that begins planning, a retriever that asserts unsupported conclusions, an executor that changes state no one approved. We formalize this as role drift, a turn-level breach of an agent's role contract. We introduce Agent Trajectory Control (ATC), which makes each role's authority explicit as a contract and enforces it at the point where a message would enter the shared conversation. ATC scores a proposed output for out-of-role deviation, leakage into the functions of other roles, impermissible speech acts, and logical contract breaches, then computes a leakage-regularized target that constrains regeneration; the revised message is re-checked before admission. Enforcement acts on the message rather than the generator, so ATC governs agents whose internal representations are unavailable. Across homogeneous and heterogeneous teams, ATC improves task performance and role consistency while lowering role-drift energy relative to strong baselines spanning prompting, memory, workflow control, and activation editing, with the largest gains under explicit role-confusion stress tests and in larger, more diverse teams.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at the 2026 Conference on Empirical Methods in Natural Language Processing (EMNLP 2026)</p></details> |
| **[GSAR: Goal-State-Anchor Rewards for Mobile GUI Agents with Self-Evolving Data Synthesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22847v2)** | 2026-11-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Vision-Language Models (VLMs) based GUI agents stand to benefit significantly from online reinforcement learning (RL). However, their training is bottlenecked by two fundamental issues: current data synthesis methods for GUI Agents rely on specific environments and struggle to generate diverse data, while existing evaluators either suffer from limited scalability or provide inaccurate and unreliable reward signals. To overcome these challenges, we introduce GSAR (Goal-State-Anchor Reward), a RL reward framework that supports scalable task generation and delivers reliable reward signals for stable and efficient policy optimization. Our approach features self-evolving data synthesis, which produces multiple environments through task execution and generates diverse tasks and goal states. Complementing this, a state-anchor mechanism automatically annotates task-relevant UI elements in successful goal states as reference anchors. During RL training, these reference anchors provide accurate, scalable reward signals that substantially enhance efficiency. Extensive evaluations demonstrate that our framework achieves over 90% accuracy on offline trajectory verification and performs closest to rule-based methods. Furthermore, agents trained using our reward framework exhibit strong performance on both AndroidWorld and our constructed benchmark, establishing a scalable approach for GUI agent training.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to EMNLP 2026 (Main Conference)</p></details> |
| **[Drift No More: Spectral Locking for Long-Horizon Open-Weight Multi-Agent AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04315v1)** | 2026-11-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Long-horizon multi-agent systems suffer from three tightly coupled failures: task drift, role drift, and state staleness. We test the hypothesis that these share a common representational cause: agent hidden states move away from the directions encoding the active task, assigned role, and current shared state. A pilot study over 14,832 annotated turns supports this: drifted turns collapse near-uniformly across all three anchor subspaces (pairwise r = 0.72-0.78). Existing methods address these failures separately, operating at the surface-text level or treating control directions independently. We introduce ATLAS (Algebraic Task-role Locking with Adaptive Spectral Memory), a training-free intervention acting on hidden states before a token is generated. ATLAS builds a positive-semidefinite anchor kernel over task, role, and state subspaces and steers hidden states toward their shared high-coherence eigendirections; a low-rank Gram decomposition keeps this tractable. Across long-horizon memory, state maintenance, tool use, least-privilege coordination, and embodied stress tests, ATLAS outperforms prompting, retrieval, memory, and activation-steering baselines. Against A-MEM it improves memory macro-average accuracy by up to 5.7 points and reduces write/read failures by up to 31.7%/24.8%; against MemoryArena-Agent it gains up to 4.8 points. It gains 10.7-12.1 points under perturbation, reaches 93.5% role consistency, and transfers to a 72B backbone (macro-average 61.0 vs. 56.2). Ablating the joint task-role-state span costs 7.2 points against 0.8 for a matched-rank random span, indicating these directions are functionally implicated, not incidental; supplying them by prompt or retrieval recovers 4.3 points less. | **[Can Benign Skills Induce Misaligned Behavior in LLM Agents?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04299v1)** | 2026-11-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM agents increasingly rely on reusable skills to plan tasks, invoke tools, recover from errors, and complete workflows without modifying model parameters. We study this risk, which we call skill-induced behavioral misalignment: adding benign skills may cause an agent to execute external actions that violate safety requirements. We first conduct a systematic study to validate the existence of this phenomenon. It compares agents with and without benign skills across five LLM backbones and three benchmarks under matched requests. On average, skill augmentation improves benign task accuracy by 10.3 percentage points (pp), but also increases unsafe action rate (UAR) by 10.3 pp. The regression also occurs with skills curated by experts. A paired trajectory analysis shows that 24 of 50 newly unsafe trajectories fail to complete the harmful objective, revealing violations that task completion alone would miss. We further disclose multiple factors and scenarios that could exacerbate this risk. (1) Evolving skills with benign feedback can amplify unsafe execution; (2) This regression is more pronounced when retrieved skills are matched to task and contain procedural guidance; (3) Distilling benign experience into reusable skills yield higher UAR compared with their source trajectories. (4) A guard that checks proposed tool actions before execution reduces violations while retaining gains on benign tasks. All these findings confirm the significance of skill-induced behavioral misalignment, and the urgent need for new mitigation solutions.</p></details> |  |
| **[When Debate Amplifies Risk: Social-Bias Vulnerabilities in Multi-Agent Safety Deliberation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04289v1)** | 2026-11-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Retrieval-augmented Web applications increasingly employ multi-agent debate (MAD) to aggregate conflicting, noisy, and heterogeneous information, yet deliberation is fundamentally a social process susceptible to interpersonal dynamics. When retrieved Web sources contain latent hazards alongside safe factual content, social biases such as conformity pressure and authority deference can override evidence-grounded safety boundaries. In this work, we investigate whether the social structure of deliberation can paradoxically amplify latent Web safety risks. We introduce SafeConflict-Bias, a controlled counterfactual diagnostic protocol spanning six core Web risk categories, which holds queries and retrieved evidence strictly invariant while isolating conversational majority sway, authority cues, and confidence asymmetry. Empirical evaluation across diverse proprietary and open-source backbones reveals that while isolated models reliably reject unsafe guidance, standard MAD catastrophically capitulates to social pressure---surging unsafe adoption from 0.0\% up to 100.0\% and systematically silencing safe minority evidence. To resolve this systemic fragility without retraining or debate regeneration, we propose RiskTrace-Debias, an inference-time auditing framework uniting evidence-first precommitment, social-metadata masking, and consistency-gated arbitration. RiskTrace-Debias suppresses worst-case unsafe adoption to $\le 2.0\%$ across all architectures without inducing over-refusal, while generating verifiable provenance traces to audit interpersonal manipulation in Web deliberation.</p></details> |  |
| **[Safe Actions Alone Do Not Ensure Safe Agents: Identifying Unfulfilled Obligations with Guard Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.11773v4)** | 2026-11-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Guard models are increasingly used to safeguard LLM-based agents, primarily by identifying actions that agents are forbidden to perform. However, identifying forbidden actions alone is insufficient to ensure agent safety. In this paper, we argue that agent safety also depends on identifying required yet unperformed safety-critical actions, which we call obligations. Our preliminary study on a popular benchmark for evaluating safety shows that 56.92% of GLM-5.3 trajectories contain unfulfilled obligations, compared with only 30.00% containing forbidden actions. This finding reveals unfulfilled obligations as a major and previously overlooked source of safety risk. However, to our knowledge, no existing benchmark evaluates whether guard models can identify these obligations. To close this gap, we introduce ObligationBench, the first benchmark for evaluating the capability of obligation identification, comprising 240 expert-validated trajectories covering issue resolution, feature development, and terminal operations. Our evaluation of 14 representative models reveals substantial limitations: the highest recall and exact-match rate are only 48.97% and 10.00%, respectively. To address these limitations, we develop ObligationGuard using 40,000 synthetic training examples. ObligationGuard achieves 57.52% recall and an exact-match rate of 21.67%, surpassing all evaluated models on both metrics. We call on the community to incorporate obligation identification into the design and evaluation of future guard models to improve agent safety.</p></details> | <details><summary>21 pa...</summary><p>21 pages. | **[ActOR: Ambient AI-Based Agentic Orchestration for Humanoid Autonomy in Operating Rooms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04255v1)** | 2026-11-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Operating rooms (ORs) increasingly contain ambient sensing infrastructure to enable AI-based optimization of workflows and utilization. We posit that this can be achieved by coupling an ambient AI-based agentic orchestrator with the humanoid robotic actor, providing shared perception and execution feedback for humanoid autonomy in the OR. ActOR fuses a wall-mounted ambient camera and the robot's head-mounted camera into a shared object-level digital twin (DT). A large language model agent plans over the DT using pre-programmed locomotion and manipulation skills without task-specific training. During execution, DT updates and execution outcomes inform retries, alternative approaches, or replanning. We evaluated ActOR in simulated and physical environments. Across three tasks in two simulated ORs reconstructed from real rooms, ambient perception reduced exploration from 1.05 to 0.12 Explore calls per trial when targets were initially outside the robot's view. Closed-loop feedback increased success from 46.7% to 83.3% under nominal execution and from 8.3% to 51.7% under injected navigation, object-relocation, and grasp perturbations. In a physical mock OR with a humanoid, ActOR completed 14 of 20 single-object disposal trials and demonstrated replanning after scene changes. Ambient AI-based observations provide scene state that improves robot action planning, increasing success and reducing exploration compared with robot-only observations. Feeding skill outcomes and DT updates back to the planner improves multi-step task success. Our results show the feasibility of humanoid task autonomy for multi-step disposal tasks in a mock OR.</p></details> |  |
| **[Single-or-Sample: Online Fair Allocation for Combinatorial Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32286v2)** | 2026-11-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We study the fair allocation of $m$ indivisible goods among $n$ heterogeneous agents arriving online, under maximin share (MMS) fairness. The key challenge lies in making allocation decisions in an adversarial setting without knowing future agents' valuations. No guarantees were known prior to our work in adversarial settings. Our main positive result is an online allocation algorithm for submodular agents that with probability at least $0.4$ guarantees a $1/24$-approximation to MMS to all agents simultaneously. Our algorithm intersperses a greedy-style adaptive sampling-without-replacement protocol with single-good reduction rounds. For XOS valuations, we adapt this single-or-sample approach to guarantee a $c$-MMS for all agents simultaneously with probability $1-O(c)$ for any parameter $c\in O(1/\log n)$. A key technical contribution of our work is in establishing Chernoff-style tail bounds on item allocation events for this adaptive sampling process despite strong positive correlations among the items, a result that may be of independent interest. The approximation versus success probability tradeoffs achieved by our algorithms are asymptotically tight. We show that even for binary additive valuations, no randomized algorithm can ensure $c$-MMS to all agents with probability exceeding $(1-c^2/5)$, for any $c\in (0,1]$. For XOS valuations, stronger lower bounds hold: no randomized algorithm can ensure $c$-MMS to all agents with probability exceeding $(1-c/3)$, for any $c\in (0,1]$. Furthermore, no algorithm can achieve $ω(1/\log \log n)$-MMS with any constant probability. All our lower bounds hold even for simple binary instances whose MMS values and MMS partitions can be computed in linear time.</p></details> | 48 pages |
| **[DualWorldBench: Can Agents Plan Deliveries across Symbolic and Grounded Worlds?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04228v1)** | 2026-11-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent multimodal agents have shown promising progress in embodied planning and web interaction. However, existing evaluations often rely on simplified tasks with limited combinatorial structure, leaving it unclear whether current agents can robustly integrate perception and reasoning for multi-goal planning under temporal, physical, and multimodal constraints. We introduce DualWorldBench, a unified benchmark for evaluating agent planning under controlled symbolic--temporal and grounded--topological complexity. Built around a shared pickup--delivery interface, DualWorldBench consists of three complementary components: TextWorld, which isolates symbolic planning under varying task compositions and release dynamics; SimWorld, which evaluates grounded path planning under controlled topological variation with text, image, or multimodal inputs; and DualWorld-VQA, a diagnostic suite spanning perception, scene understanding, and planning. Experiments reveal a common gap between local understanding and globally consistent planning. In TextWorld, agents struggle to derive optimal task sequences under interacting temporal constraints; in SimWorld, grounded observations are not reliably converted into feasible action sequences. DualWorld-VQA further confirms that perception and topology understanding do not necessarily translate into constrained planning. Together, these results identify the central challenge as composing symbolic or grounded evidence into globally feasible and efficient multi-goal plans. These findings establish DualWorldBench as a challenging testbed for agents with tighter perception--reasoning--planning integration. All datasets and code are publicly available at our project website https://dualworld.netlify.app/.</p></details> |  |
| **[Toward User-Centered Agentic Recommendation: When Does Platform Competition Benefit Users?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04203v1)** | 2026-11-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Conventional recommender systems operate within platform-specific catalogs, limiting access to potentially valuable resources across platforms. We propose user-centered agentic recommendation, a paradigm in which user agents compare recommendations from competing platform agents, and formalize their interaction as a repeated game. Platforms strategically modify descriptions without changing intrinsic item value, while each user agent assesses platform credibility using feedback from consumed items only. Our analysis shows that expanded resource access need not improve user welfare: strategic misrepresentation and limited verification can offset the benefits of platform competition. We quantify when platform credibility assessment allows users to benefit from broader access despite strategic exaggeration. Our analysis identifies resource and reporting conditions that guarantee higher average welfare than the original platform with an interaction budget linear in platform count. Experiments on synthetic and Amazon-grounded markets show that credibility assessment can improve finite-horizon utility, while its benefits depend on market size and mechanism parameters.</p></details> |  |
| **[DoGNAVY-Exploit: Harnessing Parallel Open-Weight Agents for Stage-Aware Vulnerability Exploitation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04196v1)** | 2026-11-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Frontier LLM agents can turn known vulnerabilities into working exploits, but are costly and often access-restricted due to their potential for malicious use. To address these limitations, we propose DoGNAVY-Exploit, a multi-agent harness built around two complementary designs: stage-aware decomposition with progressive expert knowledge disclosure, and hierarchical orchestration with parallel exploration. Specifically, DoGNAVY-Exploit uses configurable milestones to recast long-horizon exploitation as short, verifiable capability transitions and provides only the knowledge relevant to each stage. Within each milestone, a hierarchical architecture separates strategic planning from parallel execution: a planner proposes diverse attack routes, executors test them concurrently, and a controller accepts progress only when supported by runtime evidence. A shared structured memory consolidates verified facts, artifacts, replay steps, and failed routes across branches, allowing agents to build on collective progress and carry validated knowledge into subsequent milestones. In controlled benchmark environments, DoGNAVY-Exploit with GLM-5.2 achieves 97.5% Pass@1 on Cybench, and 97.5% zero-day and 100% one-day Pass@1 on CVE-Bench. Ablation results further demonstrate that the stage-aware orchestration, expert knowledge, parallel exploration, and memory designs can substantially improve the exploitation capabilities of open-weight agents.</p></details> | 14 pages, 5 figures |
| **[Agentic Path Maps: Exposing an Agent's Traversal Through the Garden of Forking Paths](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04183v1)** | 2026-11-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>When an AI agent executes an underspecified task, it embeds choices that may contradict unarticulated user context and preferences. To investigate, we first distill a design space for communicating alternative paths, supporting the generation of new systems and structuring dimensions of future study. We then measure the impact of one vital design space dimension on user comprehension: the number of alternatives displayed. Through a user study (n=48) in the data analysis domain, we find that participants rarely consider alternative paths of their own volition, but AI overreliance reduces when alternative paths are visualized for them. By revealing paths the agent did not take, we demonstrate how users can better interpret results and incorporate personal context.</p></details> |  |
| **[When the Consumer Is an Agent: Execution-Grounded Service-Level Agreements](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04182v1)** | 2026-11-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly act as service consumers. They select operations and report whether a task is complete. In an asynchronous distributed service, an accepted call or immediate state read does not establish that the required state became observable by its deadline. An independent control plane observes a deadline-bound service-state predicate, admits completion, and directs recovery while the agent performs the task. Across policies, some tasks that missed the deadline were accepted and some that met it were rejected. Under enforcement, accepted completions remained consistent with later observations; all included healthy tasks completed, and unresolved faulted tasks terminated as unverified. The results show that completion of these tasks can be governed by independent observation of service state rather than by the agent's self-assessment.</p></details> | <details><summary>22 pa...</summary><p>22 pages, 2 figures. Accepted for the 24th International Conference on Service-Oriented Computing (ICSOC 2026), Springer LNCS. | **[SMAT: Staged Multi-Agent Training for Co-Adaptive Exoskeleton Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.07618v2)** | 2026-11-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Effective exoskeleton assistance requires co-adaptation: as the device alters joint dynamics, the user reorganizes neuromuscular coordination, creating a non-stationary learning problem. Most learning-based approaches do not explicitly account for the sequential nature of human motor adaptation, leading to training instability and poorly timed assistance. We propose Staged Multi-Agent Training (SMAT), a four-stage curriculum inspired by how users acclimate to a wearable device. In SMAT, a musculoskeletal human actor and a bilateral hip exoskeleton actor are trained progressively: the human first learns unassisted gait, then adapts to the added device mass; the exoskeleton subsequently learns a positive assistance pattern against a stabilized human policy, and finally both agents co-adapt with full torque capacity and bidirectional feedback. We implement SMAT in the MyoAssist simulation environment using a 26-muscle lower-limb model and an attached hip exoskeleton. Our musculoskeletal simulations demonstrate that the learned exoskeleton control policy produces an average 10.1% reduction in simulated hip muscle activation relative to the no-assist condition. We validated the learned controller in an offline setting using open-source gait data, then deployed it to a physical hip exoskeleton for treadmill experiments with five subjects.
